Recently,  many  sport  scientists  have  developed  and  subsequently  used  a  different  statistical  approach  to  make inferences about potential differences between trials in the case where traditional inferential statistics deemed these differences not significant. This approach is called magnitude-based inferences (MBI). Consider the case above: while not statistically significant, is it possible that the mean difference in 60 m time as a result of warm-up A and warmup B is clinically significant or meaningful? Is the observed difference between warm-up A and warm-up B of 0.03 s meaningful? We could calculate the size of the effect, using a magnitude-based inference approach. The benefit of using this approach in exercise physiology is that, in many cases, we simply do not have a large enough sample size and/or the real effect is very small. Consider an athlete who wins an Olympic 100 m race by &lt;0.03 s as a result of using warmup B compared to warm-up A. While not statistically significant, statistical approaches like MBI or effect sizes allows the coach/athlete/sport scientist to make their own judgment along a continuum of a trial's effectiveness based on the observed effect shown, rather than perhaps rejecting the use of warm-up B because it is not statistically significant.

Shapes of Relationships: Linear, Exponential, Quadratic, Sigmoid

Exercise Physiologists often use graphs to convey important information. Graphs are simply a pictorial way of conveying data that illustrate some concept. It is important to carefully look at the graphs in this textbook, to obtain as much

information as possible from them. Most of the graphs in this textbook will be two-dimensional, but graphs can be three-dimensional. A two-dimensional graph plots the independent variable on the abscissa (also known as the x axis) and the dependent variable on the ordinate (also known as the y axis).

Graphs can convey information as simple as a frequency distribution or as complex as the relationship between load and mechanical work. Here, we will present a few graphs to illustrate some of the different concepts that are conveniently presented as graphs and show how these graphs should be interpreted. This information will help you interpret the graphs presented throughout the textbook.

Before we describe the typical relationships that are frequently depicted in graphs, it is worth considering a simple convention that makes interpretation of graphs more apparent. It is important to understand that variables can usually be considered either independent or dependent. The easiest way to think of these is that the independent variable is controlled by the experimenter and the dependent variable is measured at different levels of the independent variable. It is convention to plot the independent variable on the abscissa (x axis) and the dependent variable on the ordinate (y axis).

Linear Relationships

The linear relationship is the simplest and probably the most common relationship that can be demonstrated in a graph. Figure 1-4 illustrates a simple experiment, where heart rate (dependent variable) was measured while power output (independent variable) increased during an incremental test on the cycle ergometer. An equation can be used to describe the observed relationship between power and heart rate. This equation is shown in the graph , and has four important components, similar to the standard equation for a line (equation 1-1).

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

where y is the value on the ordinate, that corresponds to a value x on the abscissa, m is the slope or proportionality constant, and b is the intercept or offset.

The value of the information made available by the calculation of r is more obvious in Figure 1-5a. Here, the data do not fit as well to the line, and correlation ( r) = 0.5. In this case, there is still a significant correlation between the two variables, but the strength of the relationship is less than in Figure 1-5b. If the r was between -0.1 and 0.1, the presence of a line representing the data would be difficult to visualize. Another way of using the correlation coefficient is to take the square of the value (r 2 ), which provides an estimate of the variability in the dependent variable that appears to be accounted for by variability in the independent variable. Thus, in our example (Fig 1-5a) for r = 0.75, calculating r 2 (0.75 · 0.75) would equal 0.55, or only 55% of the variability in Y is explained by variability in X. This means that 45% of the variability of Y is unrelated to variability in X. This means that X is only a moderate predictor of Y.

Figure 1-4 Relationship between heart rate and power output.

<!-- image -->

Linear regression and Curve-fitting

Curve-fitting is a statistical  procedure that is useful in finding an equation that describes the relationship between two variables. Consider the data presented in Figure 1-4. The first decision to make is to determine the shape of the relationship: is it linear, exponential or sigmoidal? These terms represent different shapes of relationships, and graphs presenting these shapes are shown later in this chapter. Clearly in this case, the data are linear. It might be argued that the exception to this linearity occurs at the resting heart rate and at the highest power output.

Linear regression uses a common equation for a straight line (y = mx b; where y is the value on the ordinate and x is the value on the abscissa; m is the slope; rise over run of the relationship and b is the intercept or value of y when x = 0). This equation will find the values for the constants (m and b) representing a straight line that passes through the data with the least deviation between the line and the data. This approach is referred to as the least squares method. This approach finds the values for the constants that yield the lowest value for the sum of squares of the differences between predicted and actual values for the dependent variable. The iterative process finds the line that passes through the data with the smallest sum of squares of the differences between the data and the line. This is called the line of best fit.

The result of the linear regression of heart rate vs power output is presented in 1-4. Here, linear regression was done without including the lowest (resting) and the highest (peak power) heart rates. The line fits the data quite well. Clearly the  lowest  and  the  highest  heart  rate  have  more  deviation  from  the  line  than  the  values  that  were  included  in  the

regression. The closeness of fit of the line to the data can be quantified by calculating the correlation coefficient (r). The value of r can vary from -1 to plus 1.

Figure 1-5a Linear relationship between fat-free body mass and time to complete a 60 m dash. As fat-free body mass increases, time to complete the 60 m decreases. This result would seem counterintuitive, but the reason becomes clear once you consider the way the results are presented in this Figure. Participants of this study ranged in age from 8 to 15 years.

<!-- image -->

Figure 1-5b Linear relationship between age and fat-free mass. Not surprisingly, as children develop, they increase their fat-free mass. This results in a positive relationship between age and fat-free body mass.

<!-- image -->

When r is near zero, then there is little or no relationship between the variables. In these circumstances, it would be difficult to choose an appropriate line through the data. As the value for r approaches 1 or -1, the strength of the relationship improves, meaning the data appear to be arranged in a more linear fashion. The relevance of the negative sign is that when the values of the two variables change in the opposite direction (one increasing as the other decreases), the correlation will be negative. Note also that in this case, the slope of the relationship will be negative. When the two variables change in the same direction, one increases when the other increases, then the correlation is positive and so is the slope of the relationship. Further examples of curve-fitting are given later in this chapter, in the section dealing with graphical presentation of data.

Several software programs allow you to find appropriate constants for an equation you have entered. Even Microsoft Excel will permit curve-fitting to pre-selected equations including: linear, exponential, and polynomial

Note,  resting  heart  rate  and  heart  rate  at  highest  power  achieved  were  not  included  in  the  regression.  The  line represents the best fit across values for heart rate from the first exercise intensity (50 watts) to the penultimate exercise intensity (300 watts). This graph shows that the increase in heart rate is constant for any increase in power output. It is important to realize that each individual could be a little different, but in general a fairly good linear relationship is obtained when these two variables are plotted. However, the power output at which maximal heart rate is reached will vary considerably. Can you plot a similar graph for someone with pulmonary disease?

Curve-fitting is a valuable method to find the nature and the strength of the relationship between two variables. You will

notice in Figures 1-5a and 1-5b that although the data follow the general trend of the lines, there is a random disparity between individual values for the dependent variable and the estimate of the value given by the equation for the line. It is important to realize that the equation representing the best fit through the data is typically obtained by finding values for the constants that minimize the disparity between the actual values and the values predicted by the equation. The process of finding appropriate values for the constants is called the least squares method.

Interpretation of Linear Relationships

A graph such as that shown in Figure 1-4 illustrates a relationship. It shows that the value of heart rate is proportional to the power output, and the proportionality constant or slope of the relationship is 0.4036. This tells you that for each unitary increase in value on the abscissa, the value on the ordinate increases by 0.4036. There appears to be an offset in the relationship such that at zero power output, the heart rate would be 74.3. This is the intercept of the relationship, obtained by least squares fitting an equation for a straight line to the data.

The fact that two variables change in a proportional way and are described by a linear relationship should be interpreted to indicate that the two values are related; this may be, but is not necessarily a cause and effect relationship. In the case of the power -heart rate relationship, it is clear that cycling at a given power output necessitates a given heart rate, so there is a cause and effect relationship. However, this could just as easily be interpreted as an indirect relationship. Cycling at a given power output requires a certain oxygen uptake by the active muscles. This oxygen is delivered by the cardiac output. The increased cardiac output requires an increased heart rate. In other words, the relationship between heart rate and power exists because these variables are related through a third variable, oxygen uptake.

Although  linear  relationships  are  quite  common  in  exercise  physiology,  a  straight  line  does  not  always  fit  well  in describing the relationship between two variables. Two additional graphic presentations that are relevant in exercise physiology are described below: exponential relationships and sigmoidal relationship.

Exponential Relationships

When the value of a variable changes in proportion to its current value, an exponential relationship exists. In this case, the slope is continuously changing. The standard exponential relationship is the decay curve for radioactivity. In this case, the rate of emitting radioactivity is dependent only on the amount of radioactive material left. When the amount of  radioactive  material  has  decreased,  the  slope  or  rate  of  emission  of  radioactivity  will  decline  more  slowly.  A  key feature of an exponential relationship is that a single determinant is the underlying cause of the changing slope of the relationship.

Relaxation from a muscle contraction is considered to be exponential. An example of a muscle contraction is shown in Figure 1-6a. It can be seen that once relaxation is well underway, the high initial value for force is associated with a steep rate of decline and as the force decreases, the slope or rate of force decline, decreases as well. This graph shows the whole contraction, but only the relaxation is considered exponential. In reality, only late relaxation (shown in Figure 1-6b) should be considered exponential.

One of the unique features of an exponential decline or exponential rise to a plateau is the notion of a half-time. The time for the value on the ordinate to change to half of any value is constant, if the property is truly exponential. In Figure 1-6b, the half-time is 18.5 ms. Pick any point on the decline of force, and note that exactly 18.5 ms later, the value is half of that initial value.

Figure 1-6a Force of contraction from high frequency stimulation. This is a tetanic contraction of the rat gastrocnemius muscle, in situ. Of particular interest is the rate of relaxation, which slows as force decreases. This decreasing slope as force decreases is similar to a classical exponential decline like radioactive decay. However, it is evident that an exponential equation can only accurately describe this decay curve after substantial relaxation has occurred (see Figure 1.5b).

<!-- image -->

Figure 1-6b Exponential decline of active force. Here, the measured (blue) decline in active force from 12.7% of maximum is shown. The equation (red) superimposes on the measured data with a very good agreement. Note that to obtain this very good fit, the equation was modified with a constant (1), indicating that within the time shown, the active force appears to be leveling off at 1% of maximum force. This is probably due to very slow relaxation of some motor units within the gastrocnemius muscle. If the data were illustrated for the next 2-3 s, it is certain that the equation would not continue to fit the data because force would actually decrease below 1%. This divergence between measurement and mathematical equation fitting the data demonstrates the danger of using equations to extrapolate to values outside the measured range of values.

<!-- image -->

Exponential decline of active force . Here, the measured (blue) decline in active force from 12.7% of maximum is shown. The equation (red) superimposes on the measured data with a very good agreement. Note that to obtain this very good fit,  the equation was modified with a constant (1), indicating that within the time shown, the active force appears to be leveling off at 1% of maximum force. This is probably due to very slow relaxation of some motor units within the gastrocnemius muscle. If the data were illustrated for the next 2-3 s, it is certain that the equation would not continue to fit the data because force would actually decrease below 1%. This divergence between measurement and mathematical equation fitting the data demonstrates the danger of using equations to extrapolate to values outside the measured range of values.

A time-constant can also be associated with exponential change. The Greek symbol τ is usually used to denote timeconstant.  To  illustrate τ ,  we  have  plotted  an  increasing  exponential  relationship  in  Figure  1-7.  Here,  oxygen  uptake rises  quickly  at  first,  then  more  slowly.  This  curve  represents  the  change  in  oxygen  uptake  from  the  beginning  of exercise  at  a  moderate  intensity  for  a  relatively  trained  individual.  The  slope  of  the  relationship  decreases  as  the

value approaches a steady-state (SS). A SS is a constant level of any variable measured in the body (oxygen uptake, temperature, concentration of an ion, etc.). By definition, the time-constant is the time for the value of a variable to change to (1-1·e -1 ) where e is the natural log. This approximates 63.2%, so the time-constant is the time needed for a change of 63.2%. The following equation can be used to represent the relationship between oxygen uptake and time, as oxygen uptake i ncreases from the resting level to the SS.

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

Where V̇O2t is rate of oxygen uptake at time t; V̇O2SS is the increase from resting to SS rate of oxygen uptake, and τ is  the time-constant. Note that as t increases, the ratio of t· τ -1 increases. This ratio is a negative exponent, so as it increases in value, the expression (1- e -t/ τ ) approaches one. Therefore, oxygen uptake approaches the sum of resting and SS difference. In this example, V̇O2 reaches a SS by about 2 min. You will learn in Chapter 13 that τ changes (becomes shorter) with training. A well-trained individual will reach SS in 1 min or less. It is important to realize that it takes approximately 4 τ to approach the SS You will also learn that the exponential rise in oxygen uptake measured across the lungs does not begin at the instant that exercise begins. There is a delay due to the time required for venous blood exiting the muscles to get to the lungs.

Sigmoidal Relationship

There are  several  important  relationships  in  exercise  physiology  that  are  sigmoidal  in  shape.  That  is,  at  low  values of  the  independent  variable,  the  dependent  variable  changes  very  little.  However,  as  the  value  of  the  independent variable  increases,  the  rate  of  change  of  the  dependent  variable  increases  dramatically.  Eventually  at  higher  values for the independent variable, the dependent variable reaches a plateau where further increases in magnitude of the independent variable result in no change to the dependent variable.

An example of a sigmoidal relationship is the binding of oxygen to haemoglobin (Hb), shown in Figure 1-8. Here, it can be seen that at low partial PO2, there is virtually no O2 bound to Hb. As partial pressure increases, there is little change, until partial pressure exceeds x, then the curve becomes increasingly more steep with increasing saturation of Hb with oxygen. However, there is a maximum amount of O2 that can bind to Hb, so the rate of increase slows and a plateau occurs, where further increases in PO2 result in no further increase in oxygen saturation. You will learn more about this relationship in Chapter 9.

Figure 1-8 The relationship between partial pressure of oxygen in the blood and % saturation. Arterial PO 2 is typically 95-100 mm Hg, resulting in nearly full saturation of the Hb. The line displayed here would typically represent a line of best fit for several measurements of O 2 saturation over a range of partial pressures. This equation (inset) was determined by Severinghaus, a famous physiologist.

<!-- image -->

Normal Values for Physiological Measurements

Throughout this textbook, you will be presented with values for a variety of measurements that can be made on human subjects during exercise. Often these will be compared with resting values, or values at different intensities of exercise. It  is  useful  to  pay  attention  to  these  values,  so  you  can  recognize  whether  or  not  any  measurements  you  might  be making are yielding normal or abnormal results. For example, if you are measuring someone's heart rate at rest, and you find that the rate is 95 beats·min -1 ,  you  might wonder why their heart rate is so high. Furthermore, it is useful when considering some of the factors that limit human physical performance capabilities to use a quantitative approach. Often, the difference between elite athletes and the rest of us can be expressed by the values of their physiological measurements. Pay close attention to the units of the measurements!

When considering normal values for physiological measurements, it is convenient to qualify the physical exercise in a relative sense (see Appendix A for a table of normal values). To do this, we use the terms: mild, moderate, vigorous and supramaximal intensities. These terms are defined with respect to the intensity at maximal oxygen uptake, the highest rate at which oxygen can be taken into the body and used for aerobic metabolism. Although we use the descriptor 'maximal'  to  refer  to  exercise  at  maximal  oxygen  uptake,  it  is  possible  to  exercise  at  intensities  above  this.  When intensity of exercise exceeds maximal oxygen uptake, it is referred to as supramaximal exercise.

The choice of normalizing values (V̇O2max in this case) is somewhat arbitrary. It has recently been suggested that a more appropriate normalizing factor is the intensity associated with anaerobic threshold (Fletcher &amp; MacIntosh, 2018, Svedahl &amp; MacIntosh, 2003) . This approach takes into consideration that anaerobic threshold, which is a valuable indicator of the relative difficulty of exercise, occurs at a different % of V̇O2max in trained and untrained individuals.

Why Study Exercise Physiology?

The study of exercise physiology provides an understanding of the changes that occur in the body during exercise. This includes movement associated with simple tasks like opening a jar, walking, or climbing stairs to complex elite physical performance like participating in a hockey game or running a marathon. These changes in the body can be divided into acute (occurring at the time of the exercise and in the immediate recovery period), or chronic (occurring as a consequence of repeated engagement in the physical activity over a period of days, weeks or years).

Understanding Athletic Performance

Much of what we know about the factors that limit ultimate physical performance has to do with exercise physiology. This is true whether you consider the limits of performance in sprinting events like the match sprint in track cycling or  endurance  events,  like  the  marathon.  'Determinants  of  Success'  is  a  chapter  that  provides  a  description  of  the physiological  capabilities  of  Jake,  a  successful  10  km  runner  (Chapter  16).  Much  of  his  success  in  athletics  can  be attributed  to  remarkable  physiological  capacities.  In  contrast,  Len,  who  is  a  successful  sprinter,  has  much  less impressive aerobic and cardiovascular capacities, but has remarkable muscle properties that contribute to his success.

Although genetics is known to contribute considerably to the tremendous physiological capabilities of athletes, training is  also  important.  The  adaptations  to  regular  exercise  that  we  call  training  represent  important  aspects  of  exercise physiology.

Understanding the Human Body During Exercise

It obviously is not enough to understand the need for exercise and the physiological properties of elite athletes. It is just as important to understand the adjustments needed in the average individual as they undertake a moderate or vigorous intensity bout of exercise. These adjustments to exercise become important cell signaling processes (see Chapter 14) that stimulate the adaptive adjustments to exercise that contribute to performance enhancements due to training and to health benefits of regular physical activity.

Understanding Limitations Due to Disease

Various diseases will impose limitations on the exercise capabilities. These limitations are rarely restricted only to the realm of the disease but have wide-ranging implications for the physiological measurements we can make. For example, those suffering primary pulmonary disease have serious muscle atrophy that results in decreased strength and impaired endurance. These changes are thought to relate to a detraining effect of the lack of regular exercise, and much research is now being conducted to see if the inability of the pulmonary patients is more dependent on their muscle atrophy or is

a direct consequence of the pulmonary disease. Any disease or condition that restricts mobility will ultimately lead to an impaired cardiovascular system. The old adage; 'use it or lose it' comes to mind. Challenging the cardiovascular system with regular exercise leads to adaptations that improve cardiopulmonary performance. Inactivity or lack of exercise, whether volitional or imposed by disease, leads to the reverse; impaired cardiopulmonary performance.

Understanding Adaptations to Regular Exercise/Training

You might have come to realize that the second time you perform a strenuous exercise, it is easier than it was the first time. This is not just your imagination! Our body undergoes adaptations that are intended to resist disturbance to homeostasis when exposed to a stress. Exercise is a stress, and the body responds to exercise by adapting; changing protein content and structure to prepare the body for subsequent challenges.

The repeated performance of a given type of activity will result in adaptations that we call training. Some of the general adaptations that occur with regular exercise include the following: heart rate is lower for a given metabolic rate, stroke volume is greater for a given metabolic rate, temperature increase during exercise is less and sweating is more, fats are mobilized for metabolic substrates more easily, and the adrenergic response to exercise is blunted. Muscle fatigue is delayed as a result of the increased metabolic capacities that result from some kinds of training.

Measurement Considerations

In research and in clinical assessment, it is necessary to make measurements. This could be as simple as measuring heart  rate,  skinfold  thickness,  blood  pressure,  or  muscle  force  generation.  The  tools  and  instruments  available  for measurement in exercise physiology are quite varied and yield a wide variety of precision and reproducibility.

Whenever measurements are made, there are sources of error and, in some cases, uncertainty with respect to meaning of the value obtained. Error can occur in some measurements, even as simple as measuring height, if inconsistencies occur in the way the measurement is made. For example, consider the possibility that you have measured the height of our character, Arthur (see Appendix C) on three consecutive days. You obtain measures of 181.2, 180.7, and 181.9 cm. How can measures of height vary so much? If you go back to the video recording of your measurements, you might discover that Arthur wore his shoes during the measurements, and he had a different pair of shoes on each day. That could have contributed to the variability! If you want accurate, consistent measurements, you must be consistent in the way you make the measurements.

When we use electronic devices to obtain measurements, how do we know that the number provided by the machine is correct? quite often, now, electronics provide the measurements that in the past were obtained mechanically. For example, if you are using an electronic cycle ergometer in the local fitness centre, how do you know that the power output it gives is correct? It is important to calibrate this equipment, to be sure that the correct value is obtained.

Concepts of Validity and Reliability

The concepts of validity and reliability are tremendously important. Reliability has to do with the reproducibility of a measure and validity has to do with the meaning and correctness of a measure; does the equipment measure what it is thought to measure? Validity also has a second, closely related meaning; does the result of your experiment apply beyond the group that served as your subjects? This is called external validity. External validity is often compromised by

the nature in which the participants for a study are selected. Most studies of exercise physiology do not randomly select participants from the pool of potential participants described by the exclusion criteria for the study.

Reliability  is  assessed  by  demonstrating  that  repeated  measurements  of  the  same  thing  will  yield  the  same  result. For example, if you measure the height of Julia (another one of our characters often mentioned in this textbook) on consecutive  days  and  get:  187.9,  188.0,  and  187.9  cm.  These  measurements  are  very  close,  so  you  would  have  some confidence that you knew her height. However, in spite of the reproducibility of these measures, you do not know yet if they are valid. Reproducibility alone does not make a measurement correct! How could you be sure your measurements were accurate?

Limitations of the Research to Date

The development of knowledge in exercise physiology has come a long way in the past 50 years. Research continues, and it will still be a long time before a full understanding of the acute and chronic physiological responses to exercise are fully understood. For this reason, the information presented in this textbook is incomplete. You may have questions after you read a given section of this book. Your questions are important, and you should consider following up on them by looking up additional information. However, don't be too disappointed if you can't find the answer. It could be that we just don't know yet!

There may also be disagreements between what you read in this textbook and what you read elsewhere. This is because exercise physiologists do not all agree on a given idea, theory, or concept. Hopefully in the more controversial areas, this textbook will also provide you with the alternative view, but that may not always be the case. It is also possible that  some  long-held  interpretation  of  information  has  recently  changed,  and  a  new  paradigm  has  evolved.  As  you read this textbook, keep in mind that new research could change the way exercise physiologists view how the body functions during exercise. At this time, consider an example of how ideas can change. In this example, the history of the development of theories of muscle fatigue will be considered.

Acidosis and Muscle Fatigue

Everybody  understands  that  when  they  exercise,  or  repetitively  contract  their  muscles,  there  will  eventually  be  a decrease in the ability to perform that task. The capability of their muscles to contract decreases. I am sure you have all experienced this. We call this decrease in contractile performance, muscle fatigue. For about 100 years, it was strongly believed that intracellular acidosis was a primary cause of muscle fatigue. Recent research has suggested that this may not be true.

Consider the evidence for the persistence of the strong belief that acidosis was the cause of muscle fatigue. It probably began with an early (1807) report of the presence of lactic acid in the muscles of a stag that had been chased to the point of exhaustion before it was shot (Needham, 1971). It is not clear why a sample of muscle was evaluated for lactic acid, but the presence of a high concentration of lactic acid was associated with the coincidental existence of exhaustion. Is this cause and effect, or simply a case of two events happening at the same time?

Much later, an experimental approach was followed, and the casual observation of a correlation between fatigue and lactic acid was confirmed. AV Hill, a well-known muscle physiologist, supported the notion that fatigue in frog muscle was associated with acidosis (Hill, Long, &amp; Lupton). However, it was not until years later that a mechanism for this assumed relationship was found. It was discovered that in muscle fibres with the membrane removed (skinned fibres), acidosis decreased Ca 2 sensitivity (Fabiato &amp; Fabiato, 1978). This means that less force was observed at a given [Ca 2 ]. You

will learn in Chapter 5 that intracellular release of Ca 2 triggers muscle contraction, and that the force of contraction is dependent on [Ca 2 ]. This observation that Ca 2 sensitivity was decreased provided a mechanism for the assumed effect of acidosis in causing muscle fatigue. However, it is important to consider the circumstances and the concept of external validity. Does the fact that acidosis impairs Ca 2 sensitivity in skinned frog muscle fibres at 8C (the temperature used by Fabiato and Fabiato) mean it will also decrease Ca 2 sensitivity at body temperature of mammalian muscle? In  spite  of  occasional  experiments  that  indicated  a  lack  of  effect  of  acidosis  on  contractile  response  at  normal mammalian temperatures (MacIntosh, 1979; Pannier, Weyne, &amp; Leusen, 1970), the notion that acidosis caused muscle fatigue was generally accepted (Fitts, 1994). Then in 1995, a paper was published that shed light on the external validity of the 1978 paper. Pate and colleagues (Pate, Bhimani, Franks-Skiba, &amp; Cooke, 1995 ) demonstrated that Ca 2 sensitivity was not decreased by acidosis in skinned fibres when the muscle temperature approached physiological temperature. Later, Westerblad showed that acidosis does not affect contraction of intact mammalian muscle fibres when tested at physiological temperature (1997). It seems that researchers have made an about-face on this topic, and it is now proposed that acidosis actually helps to preserve muscle function during exhausting exercise. It has been demonstrated that acidosis delays the attenuation of  contractile  response that would otherwise occur when plasma [K ] increases during exercise (Pedersen, Nielsen, Lamb, &amp; Stephenson, 2004). It is important to keep in mind that under some circumstances, acidosis can have a negative impact on muscle, and some experts believepH interacts with inorganic phosphate to still impair muscle contraction at physiological temperature (Nelson &amp; Fitts, 2014). It is with interest that we should watch theresearch on this topic over the next several years.

<!-- image -->

Drawing by Danton Thomas, with permission

Value and Limitations of Animal Research

It might be noticed by the astute student that much of the research described above was done with animals. I am not sure where we would be with respect to knowledge in exercise and muscle physiology without the huge contribution from animal research. It is tremendously important, but as indicated above, care must be taken in interpreting that work. In particular, it should be noted that investigation of isolated tissue (like the skinned fibre experiments described above), might not have relevance to function in the body at 37°C. It is not that the observations were wrong, they just did not have external validity! This external validity (see earlier in chapter) had more to do with the temperature at which the measurements were made than with the fact that animals were used.

In the modern exercise physiology lab, animal experimentation is important. Regulations govern the way animals are used, and care is taken to minimize the stress and pain that these animals experience. The value in using animals is that

invasive measurements that would not be ethically considered in human subjects can be done with animals, whose lives are essentially dedicated to science.

National and International Organizations for Exercise Physiology

The Canadian Society for Exercise Physiology

Most academics who teach and do research in exercise physiology in Canada are members of the Canadian Society for Exercise Physiology (CSEP). This is one of the largest and most influential academic organizations in Canada (CSEP.ca). CSEP , with funding from the Public Health Agency of Canada, is responsible for originating and maintaining Canada's Physical Activity Guidelines and the Sedentary Living Guidelines (CSEP guidelines.ca). CSEP also has fostered the use of the PAR-Q for those contemplating a change in their exercise routine, including regular updates. The physical activity readiness is now evaluated with the Get Active questionnaire (CSEP.ca) a document that is available from CSEP for those who need it. CSEP is also responsible for introducing the 'Exercise is Medicine' campaign to Canada. This campaign was initiated by the American College of Sports Medicine with the goal to bring exercise prescription and physical activity monitoring into mainstream medical practice. With success in this program, more jobs will open up for those with special training in exercise physiology.

Certifications for Working as a Fitness Consultant or Exercise Physiologist

The Canadian Society for Exercise Physiology offers two levels of certification: Certified Personal Trainer and Clinical Exercise Physiologist. Information about these certifications can be found on the CSEP website: https:/ /csep.ca/.

The European College of Sport Science

The European College of Sports Science (ECSS) is a large multinational organization with academic members not only from throughout Europe but also from other countries, including Canada and the United States. ECSS was founded in 1995 and organizes a conference each year at which researchers present their latest findings. The official journal of ECSS is the European Journal of Sport Science. The vision of ECSS is 'to lead the promotion and application of world-class, multidisciplinary science in sport and exercise.'

The American College of Sports Medicine (ACSM)

The  American  College  of  Sports  Medicine,  though  based  in  the  United  States,  is  an  international  organization  of academics and practitioners of disciplines related to sports medicine. Membership now exceeds 50,000. They host an annual conference that typically has over 5000 attendees. The official journal of ACSM is Medicine and Science in Sports and Exercise. The ACSM was founded in 1954 and the journal was initiated in 1969. The Mission of ACSM is '… advances and integrates scientific research to provide educational and practical applications of exercise science and sports medicine.'

Summary

This is an introductory chapter that is presented with the purpose of giving you a glimpse of what is to follow, and to provide you with some important skills and ideas that will facilitate your understanding of exercise physiology. Learning the language of exercise physiology is very important in facilitating the learning of the concepts in exercise physiology. In some cases, you may be able to learn the meaning of unique terms from the context of its use, but in many cases, you will want to consult the glossary. Although the internet is a fast and easy way to access information, caution must be exercised when you are obtaining information from this source. Some web sites provide information of high quality while other sources can be misleading.

Your  learning  of  the  concepts  of  exercise  physiology  will  be  facilitated  by  representation  of  the  relationships  and concepts in graphic form. Some of the common relationships in exercise physiology yield straight-line relationships while  others  are  sigmoidal  or  exponential.  A  basic  understanding  of  the  concepts  of  probability  and  statistics  is important  to  allow  you  to  understand  variability,  probability,  and  the  notion  of  significant  difference.  Statistical evaluation requires measurement, and it is important to understand the concepts of validity and reliability in the context of the measurements that are commonly done in exercise physiology.

As you read through this textbook you will be exposed to a variety of measurements and you will learn to recognize the range of normal values that can be expected for these measurements. You will also become familiar to a substantial range of units for these varied measurements. These units are part of the language of exercise physiology and it is very important to become familiar with these.

One of the most interesting aspects of exercise physiology is the application to elite athlete performance. Although there are many determinants of success in athletics, including: nutrition, psychology, motor coordination, anatomy, and biomechanics; exercise physiology plays a key role in many sports and challenges. For this reason, evaluation of athletic performance becomes a fascinating way to look at the extremes of physiological capability.

Of course, where athletic performance is the epitome of the capacities and capabilities of exercise physiology, disease is  the  opposite.  There  are  many  diseases  that  compromise  the  ability  to  exercise  and  maintain  a  reasonable homeostatic condition of the internal milieu. Evaluation of these limits permits an understanding of the importance of the various physiological systems in contributing to the capability to exercise.

Now,  we  will  turn  our  attention  to  the  history  of  exercise  physiology  in  Canada.  Canadians  can  be  proud  of  the substantial contribution that has been made to the growth in knowledge and understanding that has arisen from the pioneering work of early Canadian Exercise Physiologists.

These findings will help inform exercise prescription and improve exercise tolerance and performance, from clinical rehabilitation to elite athletes.

THE FUNDAMENTALS OF EXERCISE PHYSIOLOGY

Part 2 of this textbook includes the fundamental aspects of exercise physiology. It begins with the nervous system which functions as a communication system in the body. The nervous system provides feedback to the brain and spinal cord from a number of sensors distributed throughout the body and communicates with appropriate effectors to execute the commands of the central nervous system: motor function, autonomic responses, smooth muscle contraction and relaxation and secretion of hormones. Chapter 3 is followed by a chapter describing skeletal muscle structure. Muscle is  highly  organized  from  the  sarcomere  level  to  myocytes  and  whole  muscles.  Chapter  5  presents  skeletal  muscle contraction. Exercise is the use of our muscles for expression of force and movement. Exercise physiology is the study of how the body responds to this exercise, making this an important chapter. Following muscle contraction is a chapter on energetics and metabolism. Then the support systems are covered: the pulmonary (Chapter 7) and cardiovascular systems (Chapter 8) that respond in exercise to minimize the disturbance to homeostasis. Additional mechanisms to regulate homeostasis are covered in Chapter 9. This includes heat dissipation, and regulation of ion concentrations. Chapter 10 presents the approaches taken in quantifying the metabolic capabilities of the body and Chapter 11 deals with the nutritional requirements imposed by exercise. Chapter 12 describes body composition, how it is assessed and how exercise affects the composition of our body. With a knowledge of these fundamental concepts in exercise physiology, you will be ready to tackle Part 3, the Integration of exercise physiology.

### Chapter 4: Skeletal Muscle Structure, Form for Function

BRIAN R MACINTOSH AND R. JOHN HOLASH

Brian R MacIntosh, R John Holash

<!-- image -->

Learning Objectives

After studying this chapter, you will be able to:

- Describe the general structure of skeletal muscle

- Describe the structure and function of connective tissue

- Describe the structure of motor units and relate to the central nervous system

- Describe the tendon and aponeurosis structure and function

- Identify structures associated with the neuromuscular junction

- Illustrate a single muscle fibre, myofibril and sarcomere at the microscopic level

- Describe the role played by membrane proteins

- Name and identify the function of a number of proteins that are important to muscle

Key Terms

tendon, aponeurosis, inscription, fascicle, myocyte, motor unit, connective tissue, sarcomere, cytoskeleton, myofilament, myofibril, sarcoplasmic reticulum, channels, ion pumps, transverse tubules, neuromuscular junction, end-plate, triad, terminal cisternae

Introduction

Skeletal muscle is an excitable contractile tissue that makes up 20-35% of total body mass in women and 30-45% in men (Janssen et al. 2000). As such it is an incredibly important tissue of the body. Many features of life can be directly attributed  to  skeletal  muscle: body  shape,  speech,  synchronized  and  coordinated  movement,  locomotion,  posture, breathing, controlling the direction the eyes are looking and manual manipulation of objects. With such a broad range of functional roles, skeletal muscle must have an adaptable structure that in its elementary state can respond to neural activation with that may achieve a variety of outcomes. These outcomes include: maintain force or limb position, allow shortening or lengthening for joint motion, generate power and achieve or dissipate work. In this chapter we will look at muscle structure from a macroscopic perspective, considering how the cells are aligned and connected to bones

through tendons. We will progressively zoom in on the tissues as we look at the subcellular structures and the specific proteins that make up muscle. Before we get to specific structural analysis, consider the Case Study presented next.

Case Study: Consider the Holiday Turkey

Ever since taking anatomy in a Kinesiology class, Rachel could not eat meat without relating it to muscles. She was enjoying turkey dinner with her family and picking delicately at the drumstick in front of her. There was a layer of oily under the skin. She noticed how the individual muscles pulled away from each other, maintaining a distinct shape. The Achilles tendon was connected to the large muscles on one side and ended bluntly where it had been cut away from the calcaneus. She knew that muscle fibres, too small to see separately were bundled by connective tissue into fascicles. She also recognized that dark meat, like that on the thigh and drumstick was composed of muscle fibres with high oxidative capacity, whereas white breast meat was composed of fasttwitch fibres with a high glycolytic capacity. With careful observation, she could see nerves and blood vessels disappearing into the meat near the end of the bone.

Photo 4-2  Turkey Dinner. Reading this Chapter may have just changed how you think about holiday dinners. Photo by Brian R MacIntosh (turkey by Sandra L Mann).

<!-- image -->

Gross Structure of Skeletal Muscle

All muscles are composed of individual muscle cells, also known as myocytes or muscle fibres, which are long, tubular cells,  surrounded  by  a  basement  membrane  and  a  thin  layer  of  connective  tissue  called  endomysium.  At  each  end, the muscle cell is typically connected to  tendon, which in turn connects to other tendons and bone. Contraction of the muscle fibre applies a force to the bone, which generates a moment about the joint. This moment (moment is a rotational force whose magnitude is the applied force times the perpendicular distance to the axis of rotation) can result in motion if the force is sufficient to overcome the load. Skeletal muscles are voluntary muscles in that we can decide when to cause contraction, controlling the timing and magnitude of in a coordinated fashion using the motor nerve pathways (see Chapter 3 on Neural Control). Skeletal muscle cells are striated; they have a banded appearance under the microscope, similar to cardiac muscle. This banded appearance arises from the regular pattern of sarcomeres along each cell. Sarcomeres are described later in this chapter.

In some cases, the whole muscle is much longer than any single fibre within the muscle; for example, sartorius and semitendinosus muscles, both of which extend from the pelvis to the upper parts of the shin. In these cases, individual fibres insert either into a connective tissue band called an inscription or the fibres are of varied length and occupy different portions of the full length of the muscle, interdigitating with each other. In the case of inscriptions, all fibres

of a given segment are of a similar length and there may be two or more groups of fibres separated by inscriptions. It is generally thought that no muscle fibre is longer than about 12 cm in the average sized human body (MacIntosh et al. 2006). In contrast, some of the muscles of the body may be 30-40 cm in length. In many muscles of the body, individual fibres may only be a few cm in length.

Tendons connect our muscles to bone so that when a muscle contracts, the bone is pulled via the tendon. In some cases, the muscle fibres are oriented in line with the tendon, and in some cases, the angle of orientation of the muscle fibres is different from the line of direction for the tendon. In the latter case, the muscle is considered to be pinnate (see muscle shapes below).

Muscle Fascicles, Tendon and Aponeurosis

The gross structure of skeletal muscle is essentially what you see when the skin is removed, exposing muscle. The skeletal muscles obviously have a variety of shapes: long and thin (neck strap muscles) or long and wide (hamstrings); some muscles lie flat against the underlying body structures (abdominals) and some bulge, showing their large size (gluteals). Regardless of their shape, the muscles of the body serve specific functions related to generation of force, and movement. Function may be as diverse as to maintain posture, walk, run, throw, manipulate or support. Specialized functions exist for the muscles around the eyes and within the ears. Muscles give our faces expression, and in some cases, can cause wrinkling of the skin. This wrinkling can be overcome with injection of botulinum toxin (botox), an inhibitor  of  neuromuscular  transmission.  This  practise  is  not  recommended  because  there  are  long-term  negative consequences of this treatment (Fortuna et al. 2011; Yaraskavitch et al. 2008).

A Simple Model of Muscle Contraction

Simple models of muscle have been presented in the research literature. Most of these originate in the models proposed by A.V. Hill (Hill 1953), before the sliding filament theory of muscle contraction was proposed (see Chapter 5). Figure 4-1  presents  a  common model of muscle contraction with a contractile element, in-series elastic component and a parallel elastic component. This simple model proposed by Hill has anatomical and physiological correlates that help us understand the relationship between muscle structure and function. Essentially, the contractile element represents the muscle fibres, or more specifically the myofibrils and the contractile proteins. The series elastic component represents the tendon, which lies in series between the muscle fibres and the bone. The parallel elastic component represents a series of tissues that support the muscle in the passive state: membrane, connective tissue and titin. These structures will  be  described  below.  An  important  feature  of  the  parallel  elastic  component  is  that  these  structures  provide  a resistive force as the muscle is passively lengthened above a critical length. However, when a muscle contracts and generates  force,  the  tendon  (series  elastic  component)  will  stretch,  allowing  the  muscle  fibres  and  parallel  elastic component to shorten. The parallel elastic component will hold less force as the muscle fibres shorten and the tendon elongates during an isometric (fixed end) contraction.

Figure 4-1 Hill Model of Muscle. The muscle is composed of structures that generate force (contractile component) and elastic tissues (tendon) in series with the contractile proteins (sarcomeres or myofibrils) as well as elastic tissues in parallel with the contractile proteins (membrane, connective tissue, titin).

<!-- image -->

Figure 4-2: Muscle Structure. The whole muscle is surrounded by epimysium. Bundles of myocytes are called fascicles and these are surrounded by perimysium. Each fascicle contains several muscle fibres and each fibre is surrounded by endomysium. Within each fibre there are myofibrils, bundles of muscle proteins essential for contraction.

<!-- image -->

Fascicles are Bundles of Muscle Fibres

Muscle  fibres  are  bound  together  in  bundles  called fascicles. Fascicles are surrounded by connective tissue called perimysium. Motor units are a collection of myocytes innervated by a single motoneuron (see Figure 4-2). Each motor unit  may  contain  hundreds  of  myocytes  and  these  cells  are distributed  across  several  fascicles  while  each  fascicle  will contain myocytes from several motor units.

Shapes of Muscle Relate to Function

Skeletal muscles have a variety of shapes to accommodate a variety of functions. There are 3 primary categories of muscle shape:

- Parallel fibred

- Fusiform

- Pinnate

- Unipinnate

- Bipinnate

- Multipinnate

In general, muscle fibres are either oriented in line with the line of action of the muscle (parallel fibred and fusiform), or the fibres are aligned at some angle with respect to the direction of the line of action of the muscle (pinnate). Pinnate muscle can be unipinnate where all fibres are at a similar angle, bipinnate where the fibres are oriented in two primary directions or multipinnate where fibres are oriented in more than two directions. These muscle shapes are illustrated in Figure 4-3. Each shape of muscle has some advantages and some disadvantages, as will be described below.

Figure 4-3 Muscle Shapes. Muscles of the body can be classified according to their shape: 1) parallel fibred (Sartorius), 2) fusiform (biceps brachii) and 3) pinnate (vastus medialis and rectus femoris). Source: Public Domain; modified from National Library of Medicine.

<!-- image -->

Parallel Fibred Muscle:

myofibrils and in many cases, more fibres arranged in series. Length of the muscle provides the advantage of a greater range of shortening and the potential for higher absolute velocities of shortening. The strap muscles of the neck are also parallel fibred muscles.

Fusiform Muscle:

fusiform muscle is one that is thick in the middle but narrows towards each end. The muscle fibres of a fusiform muscle tend to be aligned with the direction of pull of the whole muscle. This shape of muscle is of benefit for having a relatively large cross-sectional area but can originate and insert in relatively confined areas. An example of a fusiform muscle is the biceps brachii. Individual muscle fibres of a fusiform muscle are shaped the same as the whole muscle: wider  in  the  middle  range  but  narrow  towards  the  ends.  The  muscle  cells  achieve  this  shape  by  terminating  some myofibrils well before they reach the ends of the cell. The maximum isometric force that the muscle can generate is proportional to the cross-sectional area near the middle of the muscle and some fibres rely on lateral transfer of force. Lateral transfer of force is the transfer of force to adjacent structures (connective tissue) and also allows length change of all myocytes within a muscle even when only a few motor units are activated.

Pinnate Muscle:

A pinnate muscle (also called pennate) is a muscle that has the individual cells oriented in a direction that is at an angle to the direction of force application. The medial and lateral gastrocnemius muscles are examples of pinnate muscles. The deltoid is a multipinnate muscle, because the fibres are oriented in several directions. Pinnation, a term that derives from the Greek 'pinne' or featherlike, allows packing a greater cross-sectional area into a given volume of muscle. A greater cross-sectional area allows greater force production by the muscle. However, individual fibres are shorter, so the property that allows greater force results in compromise of slower maximal absolute shortening velocity and possibly decreased range of motion.

Retinacula Decrease the Moment Arm of Skeletal Muscle

The tendons of some muscles are attached to bone at some distance from the joint, but the tendon is held close to the joint by a fibrous band called a retinaculum (the plural of retinaculum is retinacula). This is true in particular for muscles of the wrist and ankle. One feature of this arrangement is that it allows more individual muscles to operate across a joint, but the muscles can be attached at some distance from the joint. A retinaculum offers the advantage of decreasing the effective moment arm of a muscle (see Figure 4-4). This advantage allows a greater angular range of motion with less shortening of the muscle. Another advantage of the retinaculum is that a greater angular velocity of movement can be achieved for a given velocity of shortening. The moment generated by a muscle is the product of the force times the motor arm distance. For this reason, a retinaculum effectively decreases the potential maximum moment that the muscle in question can generate.

Figure 4-4 Retinaculum. A retinaculum is a band of dense connective tissue, shown here at the ankle, that binds structures like tendons in close to the bones, thereby reducing the effective moment arm of the muscle. This short moment arm allows a faster angular rotation for a given velocity of muscle shortening. Source: Public Domain; modified from National Library of Medicine.

<!-- image -->

Connective Tissue in Skeletal Muscle

Connective tissue in skeletal muscle exists at five levels: around each cell, around bundles of cells called fascicles, around each whole muscle and connecting muscle to bone . These five levels of connective tissue are called:

- [endomysium,](https://kinesiology.ucalgary.ca/macintosh/glossary/more-glossary-terms#endomysium)

- [perimysium,](https://kinesiology.ucalgary.ca/macintosh/glossary/max-ph#perimysium)

- [epimysium](https://kinesiology.ucalgary.ca/macintosh/glossary/more-glossary-terms#epimysium)

- [tendon](https://kinesiology.ucalgary.ca/macintosh/glossary/pha-tend#tendon)

- [aponeurosis](https://kinesiology.ucalgary.ca/macintosh/glossary/5bx-dea#aponeurosis)

Connective tissue is a fibrous mesh of collagen, elastin and scattered cells called fibroblasts. Connective tissue serves several functions: it gives tissue form and guides initial formation as well as regeneration following injury. Connective tissue provides a space for blood vessels to penetrate the tissue and forms a protective sheath around muscle. The tendon is also composed of connective tissue and serves an important role in skeletal muscle function, as discussed below.

Endomysium

The endomysium is a thin layer of connective tissue around each myocyte. Individual fibrils of collagen, which run in all directions, are relatively thin (60 to 120 nm). The endomysium provides a protective layer around each cell, outside of the basement membrane. The endomysium and basement membrane provide a cylinder in which muscle regeneration can occur during recovery from muscle damage.

Perimysium

The perimysium is  a  thicker  layer  of  connective  tissue   that  divides  the  muscle  into  fascicles.  Arterioles  and  nerve branches use the perimysium to distribute throughout the muscle.

Epimysium

The  epimysium,  also  known  as  fascia,  envelopes  each  muscle  in  a  thick  layer  of  connective  tissue.  Collagen  and elastin provide  protection  for  the  muscle  while  still  permitting  the  shape  changes  needed  to  accommodate  muscle shortening and elongation.

Tendons

Tendons are also composed of connective tissue, primarily collagen with small amounts of elastin and proteoglycans. Tendons connect skeletal muscles to bone, and in most cases there is tendon at both ends of a skeletal muscle. In some cases, the tendon is relatively compliant and in other cases there is a stiff tendon. Tendons can occupy much of  the  muscle-tendon  length  or  they  can  be  relatively  obscure.  It  is  important  to  realize  that  a  muscle  can  stretch the tendon in series with that muscle and the magnitude of stretch depends on the tendonstiffness and the force of muscle contraction. The important realization from this relationship is that when a muscle contracts without changing joint angle (at either end of the muscle if it is a two-joint muscle), the muscle fibres can still shorten to accommodate the stretch of the tendon. This means the contraction is not truly isometric, and should be referred to as a fixed-end contraction.

Aponeurosis

The aponeurosis is a connective tissue surface to which myocytes of pinnate muscles are attached. In this respect, aponeurosis is like tendon. However, the function of the aponeurosis is very different from the function of the tendon. The aponeurosis must be very compliant. It accommodates the shape change of the individual muscle fibres as they shorten and become wider or elongate and become narrow. The surface of the aponeurosis must allow individual fibres to remain tightly attached to neighboring myocytes while these shape changes occur. The aponeurosis also assists with transfer of force to the tendon. However, lateral transfer of force also helps in this matter (Monti et al. 1999). Lateral transfer of force relates to the ability of a myocyte to transfer force to adjacent cells along the length of the fibre.

Blood Supply of Skeletal Muscle

Like all tissues of the body, each muscle will receive blood flow through 1 or 2 arteries and this/these arteries will go through several branchings and become the following vessels:

- Small arteries

- Arterioles

- Capillaries

- Venules

- Small veins

- veins

Vascular arborization

Skeletal muscle is important for several reasons: providing oxygen and substrates for metabolism, removing products of metabolism and excess heat, and communicating with and from muscle by hormonal and cytokine transportation. Most muscles in the human body have a primary artery providing all of the necessary blood flow. This primary artery will divide several times, with each division resulting in two vessels of smaller radius but increased total cross-sectional area. By this division, the speed of flow of blood and the resistance to flow will both decrease. Blood travels sequentially to arterioles, capillaries ,  venules and veins. This sequence is like the main water supply to your house or apartment, branching to provide water to the many sites inside that need it, (sinks, toilets, showers etc) and the drains combining to form a single waste water exit to the sewer system are like the veins of a muscle.

Arteries :

Arteries are thick-walled elastic vessels that conduct the blood from the heart to the target tissue (muscles in this case). A primary artery (sometimes two) will enter each muscle, and divide several times, each time decreasing the diameter of the branches relative to the vessel before the branch. Eventually, arteries give way to arterioles, the primary resistance vessels of the vascular system. Vasoconstriction of arteries and arterioles increases the resistance to blood flow and vasodilation of these vessels decreases resistance allowing higher blood flow.

Arterioles :

Arterioles are smaller than arteries, but have relatively thicker walls, composed of smooth muscle layers and connective tissue. Arterioles will branch a few times before delivering blood to the capillaries. Arterioles are considered important for regulating the distribution of blood flow because they have the potential for very strong constriction.

Capillaries :

The purpose of the vascular system is to provide a means for delivering oxygen, substrates, hormones and cytokines

to the various tissues and to remove substances that would otherwise accumulate in the extracellular  space. It is in the  that this function is achieved. Capillaries are thin-walled vessels that allow transport of these substances to and from the extracellular space within the tissues. The walls of capillaries are composed of a single layer of endothelial cells. Capillaries are barely large enough for red cells to squeeze in. The length of capillaries is about 1 mm on average. Exchange of substances between the vascular space and the extracellular space occurs by transport through or between the endothelial cells. In spite of being so short, the total number of capillaries in the body, when laid end-to-end would stretch all the way around the Earth (~40000km).

Venules :

Capillaries converge to form venules which are small enough and have thin enough walls that some exchange of gas, ions and small molecules can still occur here. However, the primary function of these vessels is directing blood flow from the tissues to the veins.

Veins:

Venules converge to form veins. Veins are the vessels that return blood flow to the heart from the periphery. Veins also serve as a large reservoir of blood, containing about 60% of the total blood volume. The walls of veins have smooth muscle in them, allowing to increase venous pressure. A higher venous pressure will allow a greater pressure difference between the veins and the heart, increasing blood return to the heart. Venous return is a primary determinant of cardiac output.

Lymph Vessels

In passing through tissue, blood will typically lose a small volume of fluid. To account for fluid distribution over time, this volume must be returned to the circulation. This return is accomplished by the lymphatic system, a series of thinwalled vessels that parallel the venous vascular system, emptying the contents into the subclavian veins.

The Motor Unit Organization of Skeletal Muscle

Structure and Distribution of Motor Units

A motor unit is a single motoneuron and all of the myocyte innervated by that motoneuron. Each muscle will have a number of neurons with soma in the ventral horn of the spinal cord. Each motoneuron soma in the ventral horn of the spinal cord gives rise to a single axon that exits the spinal cord to the ventral root and travels in the corresponding spinal nerve to the respective muscle. As the axon approaches the muscle, and within the muscle, it divides multiple times. Each myocyte is innervated by a single axon. The number of nerve branches, and therefore the number of myocytes innervated for each motor unit, depends on several factors: size and function of the muscle, as well as the fibre-type of the muscle fibres innervated. All myocytes of a given motor unit are the same fibre-type. Individual myocytes associated

with a given motor unit are distributed through a region of a muscle. This means that when only a few motor units are active, it is highly unlikely that several adjacent myocytes are active.

Innervation and Neuromuscular Junctions

Each myocyte receives innervation from a single motoneuron. At the axon terminus, a specialized structure permits transmission of the activation from the axon terminus to the myocyte membrane. This transfer of activation occurs via chemical communication resulting from an action potential arriving in the nerve terminal. The neuromuscular junction includes the nerve terminus, the muscle membrane immediately under the nerve terminus and the gap between these two. Figure 4-5 illustrates a neuromuscular junction.

Figure 4-5 Neuromuscular Junction. The nerve terminal ends in a node that almost contacts the sarcolemma. Under the nerve terminal, is the primary synaptic cleft (1', ~70 nm) and the underlying sarcolemma is folded, forming troughs known as secondary synaptic clefts (2' ~500 nm). Under the nerve terminal the sarcolemma has a large number of acetylcholine receptors. Beyond the end-plate region, the sarcolemma does not have acetylcholine receptors. The membrane of the nerve terminal has rows of Ca 2+ channels with nearby internal docked vesicles containing. Additional vesicles are tethered to the membrane and reserve vesicles are floating nearby.

<!-- image -->

The  nerve  terminal  is  a  specialized  neural  structure  with  membrane  vesicles  containing  acetylcholine  and  the neurolemma contains special membrane proteins that allow tethering, docking and fusion of membrane vesicles with the nerve terminus membrane. In addition to these proteins in the membrane of the vesicles there are special proteins in  the  nerve  terminal membrane that complement the special membrane proteins of the vesicles and participate in the  process  of  docking  and  fusion.  The  nerve  terminal  also  contains  mitochondria  to  provide  energy  for  the  active

processes that go on here. The membrane of the nerve terminus contains several pumps, channels and binding proteins. In addition to the fast Na + channels and K + channels, a predominant protein in the nerve terminus membrane is a Ca 2+ channel. This channel opens briefly when an action potential arrives at the nerve terminus. The resulting puff of Ca 2+ that enters through the Ca 2+ channels will cause the local [Ca 2+ ] to increase. This increased concentration results in binding of Ca 2+ to one of the specialized proteins associated with docked vesicles and results in fusion of some of the docked vesicles and the consequent release of acetyl choline into the synaptic cleft.

The End Plate

The muscle membrane under the nerve terminus and the cytoplasm immediately under that membrane have specialized structures that serve the purpose of receiving the signal from the motor nerve and transmitting that signal to a muscle membrane, resulting in an action potential. The end-plate is illustrated in Figure 4-5. A key feature that identifies the end-plate, besides the obvious nerve terminal over this region, is the deep infolding of the sarcolemma and the presence of acetylcholine receptors in this membrane. These secondary s increase the surface area of the end-plate, providing more area for the acetylcholine receptors and decreasing the chance that acetylcholine will diffuse away from the endplate region. The primary feature of the end-plate is the high concentration of acetylcholine receptors. Other areas of the sarcolemma do not have acetylcholine receptors, unless innervation for that muscle fibre is lost (Axelsson and Thesleff 1959). Denervation results in loss of activation, and activation is necessary to inhibit synthesis of acetylcholine receptors. Normally, myotrophic factors released from the motoneuron will overcome this inhibition for nuclei close to  the  neuromuscular junction. This is why acetylcholine receptors are normally located only at the neuromuscular junction.

Motor Unit Size

We know that each muscle fibre is innervated by a single axon, and that each motoneuron innervates many muscle fibres. The actual number of myocytes innervated by a given motoneuron varies considerably in the muscles distributed throughout the body. Note that muscles with fine motor control have few myocytes per motoneuron whereas muscles with less demand for fine motor control have a large number of muscle fibres per motor neuron (see Table 4-1).

How do we Know How Many Motor Units per Muscle ?

There are various methods for estimating the number of motor units in a given muscle. The most direct is to count the number of apparent motor nerves entering a muscle. This gives only an estimate since the count depends on identifying motoneurons and not counting the motoneurons. This approach requires counting the large axons to exclude sensory neurons.  Estimates  of  the  number  of  motor  units  per  muscle  are  given  in  Table  4-1  for  a  few  human  muscles.  In this Table, the fibres/unit represents an average but the actual number of fibres per unit in a given muscle will vary considerably because every muscle has small and large motor units.

Table 4-1  Numbers of Motor Units per Muscle Source: MacIntosh, Gardiner and McComas, 2006

| Muscle                    |   No. of Motor Units | Muscle fibres/unit   |
|---------------------------|----------------------|----------------------|
| First dorsal interosseous |                  119 | 340                  |
| Brachioradialis           |                  333 | >410                 |
| Tibialis anterior         |                  445 | 562                  |
| Gastrocnemius (med)       |                  579 | 1934                 |
| Masseter                  |                 1452 | 640                  |
| Temporalis                |                 1331 | 936                  |
| Flexor digiti minimi      |                  130 | 108                  |

An alternative approach to estimate the number of motor units is to stimulate the motor nerve with a progressively larger pulse (beginning with a very low voltage and gradually increasing the intensity for sequential contractions) while measuring the (McNeil et al. 2005). If the voltage begins low enough to activate a single motor unit and each increment adds only one motor unit, then motor units can be counted. A small increase in voltage that does not result in an increase in EMG amplitude is a null result, no additional motor unit was activated.

Fibre-Types in Skeletal Muscle

Typically, individual motor units can be identified as specific fibre-types. In human muscles these fibre types can be separated  into  fast  and  slow,  but  the  fast  fibre  type  has  two  subtypes.  The  classification  of  fibre  type  is  either  by contractile and metabolic factors or by myosin isoform identification. Although we classify motor units according to these fibre types, in reality, individual motor units can have properties that are intermediate between the classic fibre types. This occurs because some fibres can have two different isoforms of myosin heavy chain heavy chain, the major protein that distinguishes the fibre-types. Table 4-2 presents some typical contractile and metabolic properties that are associated with the classic fibre types.

Table 4-2  Contractile and Metabolic Properties of Muscle Fibre Types

| Property                       | Type I Slow-twitch   | Type IIa Fast-twitch   | Type IIx Fast-twitch   |
|--------------------------------|----------------------|------------------------|------------------------|
| Twitch time                    | long                 | short                  | short                  |
| Twitch amplitude               | small                | large                  | largest                |
| Maximal velocity of shortening | low                  | high                   | highest                |
| Sag                            | no                   | yes                    | yes                    |
| Aerobic capacity               | high                 | high                   | low                    |
| Glycolytic capacity            | low                  | high                   | high                   |
| Relaxation rate                | slow                 | fast                   | fast                   |
| Mitochondrial density          | high                 | high                   | low                    |
| Myoglobin concentration        | high                 | high                   | low                    |
| Fatigability                   | low                  | low                    | high                   |

Contractile Properties and Classification of Fibre Types:

The  initial  classification  of  motor  units  into  fibre-types  was  presented  by  Burke  et  al.  (1973).  These  investigators identified three distinct categories of motor units in cat muscle according to contractile properties. They evaluated several possible contractile properties, but only a few of these had distinct characteristics that allowed separation of the motor units into three types: fast-twitch, fatigable,  fast-twitch, fatigue resistant and slow-twitch. The key contractile properties  included  the  following:  force,  twitch  time,  and  fatigue  index.  The  speed  of  the  twitch  contraction  also contributes to fusion and the force-frequency relationship (described further in Chapter 5 on contractile properties). The  separation  of  motor  units  into  fibre-types  is  less  distinct  in  human  muscle  than  in  cat,  partly  because  of  the presence of more hybrid fibres (see below).

A single stimulus to a motoneuron will result in propagation of a single action potential on the axon of the motoneuron. This single action potential will follow all branches of the motoneuron and transfer a single activation to each muscle fibre associated with that motor unit. The subsequent contraction of all these muscle fibres is called a twitch. A twitch contraction will have a rise and fall of force as illustrated in figure 4-6. The amplitude of the twitch is dependent on the average fibre size and how many fibres are innervated by that motoneuron. Typically, the force of a twitch is 15-20% of the force of a high frequency stimulation (tetanic contraction). Small motor units (type I) will result in small force and large motor units (type II) will result in large force. The duration of a twitch is also dependent on the fibre type. Two classifications of fibre type can be identified by twitch contraction time or time to peak force of a twitch: fast and slow (also known as type II and type I, respectively). It is important to realize that although within a species there is a difference in contraction time between fibre types, there is also a difference between species for any fibre type. The

fast-twitch motor unit of a rat has a contraction time of 12-16 ms but the fast-fast-twitch motor unit of a human has a contraction time of 30-40 ms.

Figure 4-6 Twitch Contraction. A twitch is the contractile response to a single activation. This could be the response of a single fibre, a motor unit or a whole muscle. The twitch of a fast-fast-twitch motor unit is faster and stronger than that of a slow-slow-twitch motor unit of the same muscle. However, the force per cross-section is similar between human fast and slow-twitch fibres.

<!-- image -->

Myosin Isoforms and Classification of Fibre Types

There are minor differences in the amino acid sequence of the S1 segment that give the myosin head different kinetics. These differences in amino acid sequence are recognized as different isoforms of myosin. There are at least 9 different myosin isoforms recognized, but in adult human muscle there are only 3: type I, type IIa and type IIx. These myosin isoforms correspond to slow-twitch, fast oxidative (fast-twitch fatigue-resistant) and fast glycolytic (fast, fatiguable) respectively. Type I and IIa have high resistnce to fatigue and type IIx fatigues easily. A fatigue index can be calculated, based on the reduction in force observed over a fixed duration of time (usually, 0 to 2 or 60 min). This fatigue index is illustrated in Figure 4-7.

Figure 4-7 Fatigue Index. The fatigue index is calculated as the difference in force from initial to final force, divided by the initial force multiplied by 100 (% decline) when the muscle is stimulated at 50 Hz for 300 ms every second. In the figure, the fatigue resistant condition would have a fatigue index of 5% and the fatiguable condition would be 80%.

<!-- image -->

It  should  be  pointed  out  that  myosin  isoform  compliment  is  slightly  different  between  species.  Histochemical  and contractile assessment identifies three fibre-types. However, there are 4 myosin isoforms, corresponding to type I fibre, type IIa, type IIb and type IIx. Type IIx was not identified until 1989 (Termin et al. 1989), so prior to that, it was thought that human muscle contained type IIb myosin, corresponding with the fast-twitch fatiguable contractile properties, similar to rat and cat muscle. However, identification of type IIx myosin in human muscle (initially referred to as IId), and the realization that type IIb does not exist in human muscle is an important fact.

Sag is a unique feature of fast-twitch motor units. When motor unit stimulation results in incompletely fused tetanic contraction, the oscillations in force reach a peak early in the repetitions followed by a subsequent decrease in peak values  (see  Figure  4-8).
